People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi. Um, I came in because I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, especially at work. I’m a secondary school teacher, so there’s a lot of reading and marking and planning, and I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to get through it properly. I’ll have my pen out, laptop open, everything set up, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page.

It’s not that I don’t care about the work — I do — but my mind sort of drifts off into three other things, and I’m not actually taking in what’s in front of me. Then I have to reread the same paragraph or the same student response again and again, and it feels like I’m losing time without noticing it. It’s been frustrating enough that I thought I should actually come and talk to someone about it rather than just keep trying to push through.

I’d say it’s been really noticeable for about… eleven months or so. It didn’t feel like it suddenly appeared overnight, exactly, but around that time the school changed the curriculum calendar, and we also started having these extra after-school planning meetings.

Since then, it’s like the amount of reading, planning, and keeping track of documents increased, or at least it started feeling much harder to keep up with. Before that I could usually push through, even if it took effort, but over the past eleven months it’s been much more obvious to me that I’m losing focus and having to reread things over and over.

It’s been frustrating enough that I thought I should actually come and talk to someone about it rather than just keep trying to push through.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been really noticeable for about… eleven months or so. It didn’t feel like it suddenly appeared overnight, exactly, but around that time the school changed the curriculum calendar, and we also started having these extra after-school planning meetings.\n\nSince then, it’s like the amount of reading, planning, and keeping track of documents increased, or at least it started feeling much harder to keep up with. Before that I could usually push through, even if it took effort, but over the past eleven months it’s been much more obvious to me that I’m losing focus and having to reread things over and over.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling sad all the time, or like there’s one big obvious emotional thing causing it. But I do feel… I suppose overwhelmed is probably the closest word, at least sometimes, because when I notice I’m behind or I’ve read the same thing three times and still haven’t absorbed it, I get frustrated with myself.

And then there’s a bit of anxiety that comes from the consequences of it, if that makes sense — like worrying I won’t finish marking in time, or that I’ll miss something important in a planning document. But I don’t know if I’d say the anxiety came first. It feels more like I start off trying to focus, then I lose the thread, then I get stressed because I’ve lost time. So… yes, overwhelmed at points, but I’m not sure it explains the focusing problem on its own.

It’s been frustrating enough that I thought I should actually come and talk to someone about it rather than just keep trying to push through.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been really noticeable for about… eleven months or so. It didn’t feel like it suddenly appeared overnight, exactly, but around that time the school changed the curriculum calendar, and we also started having these extra after-school planning meetings.\n\nSince then, it’s like the amount of reading, planning, and keeping track of documents increased, or at least it started feeling much harder to keep up with. Before that I could usually push through, even if it took effort, but over the past eleven months it’s been much more obvious to me that I’m losing focus and having to reread things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious, sad, or overwhelmed during this period of difficulty with focusing?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling sad all the time, or like there’s one big obvious emotional thing causing it. But I do feel… I suppose overwhelmed is probably the closest word, at least sometimes, because when I notice I’m behind or I’ve read the same thing three times and still haven’t absorbed it, I get frustrated with myself.\n\nAnd then there’s a bit of anxiety that comes from the consequences of it, if that makes sense — like worrying I won’t finish marking in time, or that I’ll miss something important in a planning document. But I don’t know if I’d say the anxiety came first. It feels more like I start off trying to focus, then I lose the thread, then I get stressed because I’ve lost time. So… yes, overwhelmed at points, but I’m not sure it explains the focusing problem on its own.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed in areas of your life outside of your teaching job?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work, the missed follow-through has become serious enough that my department chair put me on a support plan, and at home my wife has started taking over bills and reminders because she can’t count on me to keep track of them.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, unfortunately it’s not just school. Work is where it’s most obvious, because there are other people seeing the consequences, but at home it’s started showing up more than I’d like to admit.

Like, with work, the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was… honestly pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t one dramatic mistake, more like a pattern of me saying I’d finish something, or read something, or follow up on a piece of planning, and then either doing it late or realizing I’d completely lost track of it. So now there’s this formal structure around me, which I understand, but it feels awful.

And at home, my wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she says she can’t really count on me to keep track of them reliably. Not in a cruel way — she’s trying to keep everything from falling through the cracks — but it makes me feel quite useless. I’ll have every intention of remembering something or sorting it out, and then it just doesn’t happen unless she’s chasing it or doing it herself. So yes, it’s definitely outside of teaching too, just maybe less visible until it affects her directly.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. It’s not only that I lose the thread when I’m reading something — it’s more ordinary day-to-day responsibilities as well, which is a bit embarrassing to admit.

For example, I’ll see a parent email and think, “Right, I need to reply to that properly,” and then I’ll put it aside because I want to answer carefully, and suddenly it’s two days later and I haven’t done it. Or with bills, I’ll know something needs paying, and I’ll even think about doing it, but then the late notice comes before I’ve actually sorted it. And my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way home — something simple — and I’ll genuinely intend to do it, but unless I write it down immediately or she reminds me, I can get home and realize I completely forgot.

It’s those kinds of things. Not huge dramatic memory gaps, just… normal responsibilities slipping more often than they used to, and more often than I’m comfortable with.

I don’t think I’d call it depression, no. I mean, I do get fed up with myself, and there are days where I feel quite flat after something has gone wrong or I’ve realized I’ve let something slip again. But it feels more tied to those situations, if that makes sense, rather than waking up every day feeling hopeless or sad for no clear reason.

I’m not saying I’m cheerful about it all — I’m really not. It’s been wearing me down, especially feeling like I’m not managing things I should be able to manage. But “depressed” feels like a stronger word than what I’m experiencing. It’s more frustration, embarrassment, and feeling overwhelmed when things pile up.

But I don’t know if I’d say the anxiety came first. It feels more like I start off trying to focus, then I lose the thread, then I get stressed because I’ve lost time. So… yes, overwhelmed at points, but I’m not sure it explains the focusing problem on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed in areas of your life outside of your teaching job?\n\nPatient: Yes, unfortunately it’s not just school. Work is where it’s most obvious, because there are other people seeing the consequences, but at home it’s started showing up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike, with work, the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was… honestly pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t one dramatic mistake, more like a pattern of me saying I’d finish something, or read something, or follow up on a piece of planning, and then either doing it late or realizing I’d completely lost track of it. So now there’s this formal structure around me, which I understand, but it feels awful.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she says she can’t really count on me to keep track of them reliably. Not in a cruel way — she’s trying to keep everything from falling through the cracks — but it makes me feel quite useless. I’ll have every intention of remembering something or sorting it out, and then it just doesn’t happen unless she’s chasing it or doing it herself. So yes, it’s definitely outside of teaching too, just maybe less visible until it affects her directly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're forgetting things more often than you used to, outside of just losing track of what you're reading or doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s not only that I lose the thread when I’m reading something — it’s more ordinary day-to-day responsibilities as well, which is a bit embarrassing to admit.\n\nFor example, I’ll see a parent email and think, “Right, I need to reply to that properly,” and then I’ll put it aside because I want to answer carefully, and suddenly it’s two days later and I haven’t done it. Or with bills, I’ll know something needs paying, and I’ll even think about doing it, but then the late notice comes before I’ve actually sorted it. And my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way home — something simple — and I’ll genuinely intend to do it, but unless I write it down immediately or she reminds me, I can get home and realize I completely forgot.\n\nIt’s those kinds of things. Not huge dramatic memory gaps, just… normal responsibilities slipping more often than they used to, and more often than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any low mood or depression?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it depression, no. I mean, I do get fed up with myself, and there are days where I feel quite flat after something has gone wrong or I’ve realized I’ve let something slip again. But it feels more tied to those situations, if that makes sense, rather than waking up every day feeling hopeless or sad for no clear reason.\n\nI’m not saying I’m cheerful about it all — I’m really not. It’s been wearing me down, especially feeling like I’m not managing things I should be able to manage. But “depressed” feels like a stronger word than what I’m experiencing. It’s more frustration, embarrassment, and feeling overwhelmed when things pile up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

No, I don’t think so. Not in the sense of a trauma or a major life event, anyway.

I mean, the work situation has obviously been stressful, and it’s sort of bled into everything else, but aside from that I can’t point to something like a bereavement, accident, or anything frightening that happened. I suppose I hesitate because “stressful” can cover a lot, can’t it? There have been normal stresses — family stuff, money admin, the usual pressures — but nothing that stands out as a particular event in the last year. So no, not really.

Not really, no — at least not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. I don’t think I get the sort of racing heart or chest tightness you’re describing. I’m not completely sure, to be honest. I haven’t really tracked it in any proper way, so it’s hard to say whether there’s been a clear change over the last eleven months specifically.

I’m not sure I’d say a decrease in motivation exactly. It’s a bit hard to separate out, because with things I normally enjoy, I don’t think I’ve stopped wanting to do them in the sense of not caring about them anymore.

It’s more that sometimes getting started feels like more of a hurdle than it should, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Oh, I’d like to watch that programme,” or “I should message someone back,” or “It would be nice to do something at the weekend,” but then the thought sits there and I don’t necessarily act on it straight away. But I’m not sure that’s the same as losing motivation or enjoyment.

When I actually do end up doing something I like, I can still enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say there’s been a clear loss of interest in things. I haven’t noticed a big change where I suddenly don’t want to do anything I used to like. It’s more… I don’t know, a bit patchy or inconsistent, maybe. But not a definite decrease in motivation, no.

It’s not only that I lose the thread when I’m reading something — it’s more ordinary day-to-day responsibilities as well, which is a bit embarrassing to admit.\n\nFor example, I’ll see a parent email and think, “Right, I need to reply to that properly,” and then I’ll put it aside because I want to answer carefully, and suddenly it’s two days later and I haven’t done it. Or with bills, I’ll know something needs paying, and I’ll even think about doing it, but then the late notice comes before I’ve actually sorted it. And my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way home — something simple — and I’ll genuinely intend to do it, but unless I write it down immediately or she reminds me, I can get home and realize I completely forgot.\n\nIt’s those kinds of things. Not huge dramatic memory gaps, just… normal responsibilities slipping more often than they used to, and more often than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any low mood or depression?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it depression, no. I mean, I do get fed up with myself, and there are days where I feel quite flat after something has gone wrong or I’ve realized I’ve let something slip again. But it feels more tied to those situations, if that makes sense, rather than waking up every day feeling hopeless or sad for no clear reason.\n\nI’m not saying I’m cheerful about it all — I’m really not. It’s been wearing me down, especially feeling like I’m not managing things I should be able to manage. But “depressed” feels like a stronger word than what I’m experiencing. It’s more frustration, embarrassment, and feeling overwhelmed when things pile up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events in the last year, aside from the changes at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in the sense of a trauma or a major life event, anyway.\n\nI mean, the work situation has obviously been stressful, and it’s sort of bled into everything else, but aside from that I can’t point to something like a bereavement, accident, or anything frightening that happened. Nothing like that. It’s more been this gradual build-up of ordinary things feeling harder to keep on top of, rather than one big event that set it off.\n\nI suppose I hesitate because “stressful” can cover a lot, can’t it? There have been normal stresses — family stuff, money admin, the usual pressures — but nothing that stands out as a particular event in the last year. So no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice any physical sensations, like a racing heart or tightness in your chest, when you're feeling overwhelmed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. I don’t think I get the sort of racing heart or chest tightness you’re describing. When I’m overwhelmed, it’s more that I feel mentally overloaded, like there are too many things competing for my attention, rather than something very physical happening in my body.\n\nI suppose there may be times when I’m tense without really registering it, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had episodes where I thought, “My heart’s pounding,” or “My chest feels tight.” If it has happened, it hasn’t been prominent enough for me to remember it as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the past eleven months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, to be honest. I haven’t really tracked it in any proper way, so it’s hard to say whether there’s been a clear change over the last eleven months specifically.\n\nI mean, there have probably been nights where I’ve gone to bed later than I meant to, or where I’ve been lying there thinking about things I still need to do, but I don’t know if that’s new or just something I notice more when life feels a bit more pressured. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, my sleep has dramatically changed,” because I don’t think that’s quite right.\n\nSo… I suppose the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a clear pattern. It might be a bit inconsistent at times, but I couldn’t point to a definite change in my sleep and say that started eleven months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your motivation to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a decrease in motivation exactly. It’s a bit hard to separate out, because with things I normally enjoy, I don’t think I’ve stopped wanting to do them in the sense of not caring about them anymore.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes getting started feels like more of a hurdle than it should, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Oh, I’d like to watch that programme,” or “I should message someone back,” or “It would be nice to do something at the weekend,” but then the thought sits there and I don’t necessarily act on it straight away. But I’m not sure that’s the same as losing motivation or enjoyment.\n\nWhen I actually do end up doing something I like, I can still enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say there’s been a clear loss of interest in things. I haven’t noticed a big change where I suddenly don’t want to do anything I used to like. It’s more… I don’t know, a bit patchy or inconsistent, maybe. But not a definite decrease in motivation, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… unfortunately, yes. I think that’s probably one of the areas where it’s become harder to brush off as just me being a bit disorganised.

With friends and family, it’s not that I’ve fallen out with people exactly, but it has definitely put strain on things. At home especially, my wife has started taking over more of the bills and reminders and general keeping-track-of-life things, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll remember or follow through consistently. And I don’t blame her, because there have been enough times where I’ve said, “Yes, I’ll sort that,” and then it just hasn’t happened, or I’ve remembered too late, or I’ve needed several reminders. But it still feels horrible, because it puts her in this position of having to manage me a bit, which isn’t fair.

At work it’s been more formal, I suppose. I’m a secondary school teacher, and the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was quite embarrassing, honestly. It wasn’t one single dramatic thing, more a pattern of things not being completed when I said they would be, or needing chasing, and eventually it became something they had to address properly.

So yes, I’d say it has affected relationships — particularly with my wife, and professionally with colleagues as well. It’s not that people have stopped caring about me, but I can tell they’re more cautious about relying on me, and that’s difficult.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, I came in because I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, especially at work. I’m a secondary school teacher, so there’s a lot of reading and marking and planning, and I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to get through it properly. I’ll have my pen out, laptop open, everything set up, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about the work — I do — but my mind sort of drifts off into three other things, and I’m not actually taking in what’s in front of me. Then I have to reread the same paragraph or the same student response again and again, and it feels like I’m losing time without noticing it. It’s been frustrating enough that I thought I should actually come and talk to someone about it rather than just keep trying to push through.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been really noticeable for about… eleven months or so. It didn’t feel like it suddenly appeared overnight, exactly, but around that time the school changed the curriculum calendar, and we also started having these extra after-school planning meetings.\n\nSince then, it’s like the amount of reading, planning, and keeping track of documents increased, or at least it started feeling much harder to keep up with. Before that I could usually push through, even if it took effort, but over the past eleven months it’s been much more obvious to me that I’m losing focus and having to reread things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious, sad, or overwhelmed during this period of difficulty with focusing?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling sad all the time, or like there’s one big obvious emotional thing causing it. But I do feel… I suppose overwhelmed is probably the closest word, at least sometimes, because when I notice I’m behind or I’ve read the same thing three times and still haven’t absorbed it, I get frustrated with myself.\n\nAnd then there’s a bit of anxiety that comes from the consequences of it, if that makes sense — like worrying I won’t finish marking in time, or that I’ll miss something important in a planning document. But I don’t know if I’d say the anxiety came first. It feels more like I start off trying to focus, then I lose the thread, then I get stressed because I’ve lost time. So… yes, overwhelmed at points, but I’m not sure it explains the focusing problem on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed in areas of your life outside of your teaching job?\n\nPatient: Yes, unfortunately it’s not just school. Work is where it’s most obvious, because there are other people seeing the consequences, but at home it’s started showing up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike, with work, the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was… honestly pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t one dramatic mistake, more like a pattern of me saying I’d finish something, or read something, or follow up on a piece of planning, and then either doing it late or realizing I’d completely lost track of it. So now there’s this formal structure around me, which I understand, but it feels awful.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she says she can’t really count on me to keep track of them reliably. Not in a cruel way — she’s trying to keep everything from falling through the cracks — but it makes me feel quite useless. I’ll have every intention of remembering something or sorting it out, and then it just doesn’t happen unless she’s chasing it or doing it herself. So yes, it’s definitely outside of teaching too, just maybe less visible until it affects her directly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're forgetting things more often than you used to, outside of just losing track of what you're reading or doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s not only that I lose the thread when I’m reading something — it’s more ordinary day-to-day responsibilities as well, which is a bit embarrassing to admit.\n\nFor example, I’ll see a parent email and think, “Right, I need to reply to that properly,” and then I’ll put it aside because I want to answer carefully, and suddenly it’s two days later and I haven’t done it. Or with bills, I’ll know something needs paying, and I’ll even think about doing it, but then the late notice comes before I’ve actually sorted it. And my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way home — something simple — and I’ll genuinely intend to do it, but unless I write it down immediately or she reminds me, I can get home and realize I completely forgot.\n\nIt’s those kinds of things. Not huge dramatic memory gaps, just… normal responsibilities slipping more often than they used to, and more often than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any low mood or depression?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it depression, no. I mean, I do get fed up with myself, and there are days where I feel quite flat after something has gone wrong or I’ve realized I’ve let something slip again. But it feels more tied to those situations, if that makes sense, rather than waking up every day feeling hopeless or sad for no clear reason.\n\nI’m not saying I’m cheerful about it all — I’m really not. It’s been wearing me down, especially feeling like I’m not managing things I should be able to manage. But “depressed” feels like a stronger word than what I’m experiencing. It’s more frustration, embarrassment, and feeling overwhelmed when things pile up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events in the last year, aside from the changes at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in the sense of a trauma or a major life event, anyway.\n\nI mean, the work situation has obviously been stressful, and it’s sort of bled into everything else, but aside from that I can’t point to something like a bereavement, accident, or anything frightening that happened. Nothing like that. It’s more been this gradual build-up of ordinary things feeling harder to keep on top of, rather than one big event that set it off.\n\nI suppose I hesitate because “stressful” can cover a lot, can’t it? There have been normal stresses — family stuff, money admin, the usual pressures — but nothing that stands out as a particular event in the last year. So no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice any physical sensations, like a racing heart or tightness in your chest, when you're feeling overwhelmed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. I don’t think I get the sort of racing heart or chest tightness you’re describing. When I’m overwhelmed, it’s more that I feel mentally overloaded, like there are too many things competing for my attention, rather than something very physical happening in my body.\n\nI suppose there may be times when I’m tense without really registering it, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had episodes where I thought, “My heart’s pounding,” or “My chest feels tight.” If it has happened, it hasn’t been prominent enough for me to remember it as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the past eleven months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, to be honest. I haven’t really tracked it in any proper way, so it’s hard to say whether there’s been a clear change over the last eleven months specifically.\n\nI mean, there have probably been nights where I’ve gone to bed later than I meant to, or where I’ve been lying there thinking about things I still need to do, but I don’t know if that’s new or just something I notice more when life feels a bit more pressured. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, my sleep has dramatically changed,” because I don’t think that’s quite right.\n\nSo… I suppose the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a clear pattern. It might be a bit inconsistent at times, but I couldn’t point to a definite change in my sleep and say that started eleven months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your motivation to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a decrease in motivation exactly. It’s a bit hard to separate out, because with things I normally enjoy, I don’t think I’ve stopped wanting to do them in the sense of not caring about them anymore.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes getting started feels like more of a hurdle than it should, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Oh, I’d like to watch that programme,” or “I should message someone back,” or “It would be nice to do something at the weekend,” but then the thought sits there and I don’t necessarily act on it straight away. But I’m not sure that’s the same as losing motivation or enjoyment.\n\nWhen I actually do end up doing something I like, I can still enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say there’s been a clear loss of interest in things. I haven’t noticed a big change where I suddenly don’t want to do anything I used to like. It’s more… I don’t know, a bit patchy or inconsistent, maybe. But not a definite decrease in motivation, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members due to your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, yes. I think that’s probably one of the areas where it’s become harder to brush off as just me being a bit disorganised.\n\nWith friends and family, it’s not that I’ve fallen out with people exactly, but it has definitely put strain on things. And I don’t blame her, because there have been enough times where I’ve said, “Yes, I’ll sort that,” and then it just hasn’t happened, or I’ve remembered too late, or I’ve needed several reminders. But it still feels horrible, because it puts her in this position of having to manage me a bit, which isn’t fair.\n\nAt work it’s been more formal, I suppose. I’m a secondary school teacher, and the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was quite embarrassing, honestly. It wasn’t one single dramatic thing, more a pattern of things not being completed when I said they would be, or needing chasing, and eventually it became something they had to address properly.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has affected relationships — particularly with my wife, and professionally with colleagues as well. It’s not that people have stopped caring about me, but I can tell they’re more cautious about relying on me, and that’s difficult.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any difficulties with decision-making or problem-solving?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. It’s a bit hard to separate out, because with things I normally enjoy, I don’t think I’ve stopped wanting to do them in the sense of not caring about them anymore.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes getting started feels like more of a hurdle than it should, if that makes sense.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed a very clear problem-solving issue in the sense of, you know, being unable to work things out or not understanding what needs to happen.

Decision-making… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d want to describe it as a major thing. I can be a bit slow to decide if there are lots of small options, or if it feels like I need to pick the “right” way to do something. I might go back and forth in my head for longer than is really necessary. But I don’t know if that’s a separate difficulty or just me overcomplicating things a bit.

With practical problems, once I’m actually focused on them, I think I can usually solve them reasonably well. It’s more that I might take a while to settle on where to start, or I might leave a decision sitting there because I’m not quite ready to commit to it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly can’t make decisions or solve problems.

So… yes and no, I suppose. There might be some hesitation or getting stuck on the starting point, but I haven’t really identified it as a distinct problem on its own.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.8, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, I came in because I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, especially at work. I’m a secondary school teacher, so there’s a lot of reading and marking and planning, and I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to get through it properly. I’ll have my pen out, laptop open, everything set up, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about the work — I do — but my mind sort of drifts off into three other things, and I’m not actually taking in what’s in front of me. Then I have to reread the same paragraph or the same student response again and again, and it feels like I’m losing time without noticing it. It’s been frustrating enough that I thought I should actually come and talk to someone about it rather than just keep trying to push through.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been really noticeable for about… eleven months or so. It didn’t feel like it suddenly appeared overnight, exactly, but around that time the school changed the curriculum calendar, and we also started having these extra after-school planning meetings.\n\nSince then, it’s like the amount of reading, planning, and keeping track of documents increased, or at least it started feeling much harder to keep up with. Before that I could usually push through, even if it took effort, but over the past eleven months it’s been much more obvious to me that I’m losing focus and having to reread things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious, sad, or overwhelmed during this period of difficulty with focusing?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling sad all the time, or like there’s one big obvious emotional thing causing it. But I do feel… I suppose overwhelmed is probably the closest word, at least sometimes, because when I notice I’m behind or I’ve read the same thing three times and still haven’t absorbed it, I get frustrated with myself.\n\nAnd then there’s a bit of anxiety that comes from the consequences of it, if that makes sense — like worrying I won’t finish marking in time, or that I’ll miss something important in a planning document. But I don’t know if I’d say the anxiety came first. It feels more like I start off trying to focus, then I lose the thread, then I get stressed because I’ve lost time. So… yes, overwhelmed at points, but I’m not sure it explains the focusing problem on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed in areas of your life outside of your teaching job?\n\nPatient: Yes, unfortunately it’s not just school. Work is where it’s most obvious, because there are other people seeing the consequences, but at home it’s started showing up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike, with work, the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was… honestly pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t one dramatic mistake, more like a pattern of me saying I’d finish something, or read something, or follow up on a piece of planning, and then either doing it late or realizing I’d completely lost track of it. So now there’s this formal structure around me, which I understand, but it feels awful.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she says she can’t really count on me to keep track of them reliably. Not in a cruel way — she’s trying to keep everything from falling through the cracks — but it makes me feel quite useless. I’ll have every intention of remembering something or sorting it out, and then it just doesn’t happen unless she’s chasing it or doing it herself. So yes, it’s definitely outside of teaching too, just maybe less visible until it affects her directly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're forgetting things more often than you used to, outside of just losing track of what you're reading or doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s not only that I lose the thread when I’m reading something — it’s more ordinary day-to-day responsibilities as well, which is a bit embarrassing to admit.\n\nFor example, I’ll see a parent email and think, “Right, I need to reply to that properly,” and then I’ll put it aside because I want to answer carefully, and suddenly it’s two days later and I haven’t done it. Or with bills, I’ll know something needs paying, and I’ll even think about doing it, but then the late notice comes before I’ve actually sorted it. And my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way home — something simple — and I’ll genuinely intend to do it, but unless I write it down immediately or she reminds me, I can get home and realize I completely forgot.\n\nIt’s those kinds of things. Not huge dramatic memory gaps, just… normal responsibilities slipping more often than they used to, and more often than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any low mood or depression?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it depression, no. I mean, I do get fed up with myself, and there are days where I feel quite flat after something has gone wrong or I’ve realized I’ve let something slip again. But it feels more tied to those situations, if that makes sense, rather than waking up every day feeling hopeless or sad for no clear reason.\n\nI’m not saying I’m cheerful about it all — I’m really not. It’s been wearing me down, especially feeling like I’m not managing things I should be able to manage. But “depressed” feels like a stronger word than what I’m experiencing. It’s more frustration, embarrassment, and feeling overwhelmed when things pile up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events in the last year, aside from the changes at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in the sense of a trauma or a major life event, anyway.\n\nI mean, the work situation has obviously been stressful, and it’s sort of bled into everything else, but aside from that I can’t point to something like a bereavement, accident, or anything frightening that happened. Nothing like that. It’s more been this gradual build-up of ordinary things feeling harder to keep on top of, rather than one big event that set it off.\n\nI suppose I hesitate because “stressful” can cover a lot, can’t it? There have been normal stresses — family stuff, money admin, the usual pressures — but nothing that stands out as a particular event in the last year. So no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice any physical sensations, like a racing heart or tightness in your chest, when you're feeling overwhelmed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. I don’t think I get the sort of racing heart or chest tightness you’re describing. When I’m overwhelmed, it’s more that I feel mentally overloaded, like there are too many things competing for my attention, rather than something very physical happening in my body.\n\nI suppose there may be times when I’m tense without really registering it, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had episodes where I thought, “My heart’s pounding,” or “My chest feels tight.” If it has happened, it hasn’t been prominent enough for me to remember it as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the past eleven months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, to be honest. I haven’t really tracked it in any proper way, so it’s hard to say whether there’s been a clear change over the last eleven months specifically.\n\nI mean, there have probably been nights where I’ve gone to bed later than I meant to, or where I’ve been lying there thinking about things I still need to do, but I don’t know if that’s new or just something I notice more when life feels a bit more pressured. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, my sleep has dramatically changed,” because I don’t think that’s quite right.\n\nSo… I suppose the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a clear pattern. It might be a bit inconsistent at times, but I couldn’t point to a definite change in my sleep and say that started eleven months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your motivation to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a decrease in motivation exactly. It’s a bit hard to separate out, because with things I normally enjoy, I don’t think I’ve stopped wanting to do them in the sense of not caring about them anymore.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes getting started feels like more of a hurdle than it should, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Oh, I’d like to watch that programme,” or “I should message someone back,” or “It would be nice to do something at the weekend,” but then the thought sits there and I don’t necessarily act on it straight away. But I’m not sure that’s the same as losing motivation or enjoyment.\n\nWhen I actually do end up doing something I like, I can still enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say there’s been a clear loss of interest in things. I haven’t noticed a big change where I suddenly don’t want to do anything I used to like. It’s more… I don’t know, a bit patchy or inconsistent, maybe. When I’m overwhelmed, it’s more that I feel mentally overloaded, like there are too many things competing for my attention, rather than something very physical happening in my body.\n\nI suppose there may be times when I’m tense without really registering it, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had episodes where I thought, “My heart’s pounding,” or “My chest feels tight.” If it has happened, it hasn’t been prominent enough for me to remember it as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the past eleven months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, to be honest. I haven’t really tracked it in any proper way, so it’s hard to say whether there’s been a clear change over the last eleven months specifically.\n\nI mean, there have probably been nights where I’ve gone to bed later than I meant to, or where I’ve been lying there thinking about things I still need to do, but I don’t know if that’s new or just something I notice more when life feels a bit more pressured. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, my sleep has dramatically changed,” because I don’t think that’s quite right.\n\nSo… I suppose the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a clear pattern. It might be a bit inconsistent at times, but I couldn’t point to a definite change in my sleep and say that started eleven months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your motivation to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a decrease in motivation exactly. It’s a bit hard to separate out, because with things I normally enjoy, I don’t think I’ve stopped wanting to do them in the sense of not caring about them anymore.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes getting started feels like more of a hurdle than it should, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Oh, I’d like to watch that programme,” or “I should message someone back,” or “It would be nice to do something at the weekend,” but then the thought sits there and I don’t necessarily act on it straight away. But I’m not sure that’s the same as losing motivation or enjoyment.\n\nWhen I actually do end up doing something I like, I can still enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say there’s been a clear loss of interest in things. I haven’t noticed a big change where I suddenly don’t want to do anything I used to like. It’s more… I don’t know, a bit patchy or inconsistent, maybe. But not a definite decrease in motivation, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members due to your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, yes. I think that’s probably one of the areas where it’s become harder to brush off as just me being a bit disorganised.\n\nWith friends and family, it’s not that I’ve fallen out with people exactly, but it has definitely put strain on things. At home especially, my wife has started taking over more of the bills and reminders and general keeping-track-of-life things, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll remember or follow through consistently. And I don’t blame her, because there have been enough times where I’ve said, “Yes, I’ll sort that,” and then it just hasn’t happened, or I’ve remembered too late, or I’ve needed several reminders. But it still feels horrible, because it puts her in this position of having to manage me a bit, which isn’t fair.\n\nAt work it’s been more formal, I suppose. I’m a secondary school teacher, and the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was quite embarrassing, honestly. It wasn’t one single dramatic thing, more a pattern of things not being completed when I said they would be, or needing chasing, and eventually it became something they had to address properly.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has affected relationships — particularly with my wife, and professionally with colleagues as well. It’s not that people have stopped caring about me, but I can tell they’re more cautious about relying on me, and that’s difficult.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with decision-making or problem-solving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed a very clear problem-solving issue in the sense of, you know, being unable to work things out or not understanding what needs to happen.\n\nDecision-making… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d want to describe it as a major thing. I can be a bit slow to decide if there are lots of small options, or if it feels like I need to pick the “right” way to do something. I might go back and forth in my head for longer than is really necessary. But I don’t know if that’s a separate difficulty or just me overcomplicating things a bit.\n\nWith practical problems, once I’m actually focused on them, I think I can usually solve them reasonably well. It’s more that I might take a while to settle on where to start, or I might leave a decision sitting there because I’m not quite ready to commit to it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly can’t make decisions or solve problems.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. There might be some hesitation or getting stuck on the starting point, but I haven’t really identified it as a distinct problem on its own.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No, I don’t think so. Not in any obvious or significant way, anyway.

I mean, I still feel like I can learn new information when I need to. If someone explains something to me properly, or if I sit down and actually engage with it, I can understand it and take it in. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that my memory or ability to learn has suddenly got worse, or that I’m unable to grasp new things.

Sometimes I might need to go over something more than once, but I think that’s fairly normal depending on how much is going on or how interested I am in the material. I haven’t had a sense of, “Oh, I used to be able to learn things and now I can’t.” It’s more that some information sticks better than other information, especially if it feels immediately relevant or I’ve properly focused on it.

So overall, no — I haven’t noticed a significant decline in learning new information.

I’ll have my pen out, laptop open, everything set up, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about the work — I do — but my mind sort of drifts off into three other things, and I’m not actually taking in what’s in front of me. Then I have to reread the same paragraph or the same student response again and again, and it feels like I’m losing time without noticing it. It’s been frustrating enough that I thought I should actually come and talk to someone about it rather than just keep trying to push through.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been really noticeable for about… eleven months or so. It didn’t feel like it suddenly appeared overnight, exactly, but around that time the school changed the curriculum calendar, and we also started having these extra after-school planning meetings.\n\nSince then, it’s like the amount of reading, planning, and keeping track of documents increased, or at least it started feeling much harder to keep up with. Before that I could usually push through, even if it took effort, but over the past eleven months it’s been much more obvious to me that I’m losing focus and having to reread things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious, sad, or overwhelmed during this period of difficulty with focusing?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling sad all the time, or like there’s one big obvious emotional thing causing it. But I do feel… I suppose overwhelmed is probably the closest word, at least sometimes, because when I notice I’m behind or I’ve read the same thing three times and still haven’t absorbed it, I get frustrated with myself.\n\nAnd then there’s a bit of anxiety that comes from the consequences of it, if that makes sense — like worrying I won’t finish marking in time, or that I’ll miss something important in a planning document. But I don’t know if I’d say the anxiety came first. It feels more like I start off trying to focus, then I lose the thread, then I get stressed because I’ve lost time. So… yes, overwhelmed at points, but I’m not sure it explains the focusing problem on its own.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focus or feeling overwhelmed in areas of your life outside of your teaching job?\n\nPatient: Yes, unfortunately it’s not just school. Work is where it’s most obvious, because there are other people seeing the consequences, but at home it’s started showing up more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike, with work, the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was… honestly pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t one dramatic mistake, more like a pattern of me saying I’d finish something, or read something, or follow up on a piece of planning, and then either doing it late or realizing I’d completely lost track of it. So now there’s this formal structure around me, which I understand, but it feels awful.\n\nAnd at home, my wife has started taking over things like bills and reminders because she says she can’t really count on me to keep track of them reliably. Not in a cruel way — she’s trying to keep everything from falling through the cracks — but it makes me feel quite useless. I’ll have every intention of remembering something or sorting it out, and then it just doesn’t happen unless she’s chasing it or doing it herself. So yes, it’s definitely outside of teaching too, just maybe less visible until it affects her directly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're forgetting things more often than you used to, outside of just losing track of what you're reading or doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It’s not only that I lose the thread when I’m reading something — it’s more ordinary day-to-day responsibilities as well, which is a bit embarrassing to admit.\n\nFor example, I’ll see a parent email and think, “Right, I need to reply to that properly,” and then I’ll put it aside because I want to answer carefully, and suddenly it’s two days later and I haven’t done it. Or with bills, I’ll know something needs paying, and I’ll even think about doing it, but then the late notice comes before I’ve actually sorted it. And my wife might ask me to pick something up on the way home — something simple — and I’ll genuinely intend to do it, but unless I write it down immediately or she reminds me, I can get home and realize I completely forgot.\n\nIt’s those kinds of things. Not huge dramatic memory gaps, just… normal responsibilities slipping more often than they used to, and more often than I’m comfortable with.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any low mood or depression?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it depression, no. I mean, I do get fed up with myself, and there are days where I feel quite flat after something has gone wrong or I’ve realized I’ve let something slip again. But it feels more tied to those situations, if that makes sense, rather than waking up every day feeling hopeless or sad for no clear reason.\n\nI’m not saying I’m cheerful about it all — I’m really not. It’s been wearing me down, especially feeling like I’m not managing things I should be able to manage. But “depressed” feels like a stronger word than what I’m experiencing. It’s more frustration, embarrassment, and feeling overwhelmed when things pile up.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any particularly stressful or traumatic events in the last year, aside from the changes at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in the sense of a trauma or a major life event, anyway.\n\nI mean, the work situation has obviously been stressful, and it’s sort of bled into everything else, but aside from that I can’t point to something like a bereavement, accident, or anything frightening that happened. Nothing like that. It’s more been this gradual build-up of ordinary things feeling harder to keep on top of, rather than one big event that set it off.\n\nI suppose I hesitate because “stressful” can cover a lot, can’t it? There have been normal stresses — family stuff, money admin, the usual pressures — but nothing that stands out as a particular event in the last year. So no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice any physical sensations, like a racing heart or tightness in your chest, when you're feeling overwhelmed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in a way that I’ve clearly noticed. I don’t think I get the sort of racing heart or chest tightness you’re describing. When I’m overwhelmed, it’s more that I feel mentally overloaded, like there are too many things competing for my attention, rather than something very physical happening in my body.\n\nI suppose there may be times when I’m tense without really registering it, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had episodes where I thought, “My heart’s pounding,” or “My chest feels tight.” If it has happened, it hasn’t been prominent enough for me to remember it as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the past eleven months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, to be honest. I haven’t really tracked it in any proper way, so it’s hard to say whether there’s been a clear change over the last eleven months specifically.\n\nI mean, there have probably been nights where I’ve gone to bed later than I meant to, or where I’ve been lying there thinking about things I still need to do, but I don’t know if that’s new or just something I notice more when life feels a bit more pressured. I wouldn’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, my sleep has dramatically changed,” because I don’t think that’s quite right.\n\nSo… I suppose the honest answer is I haven’t noticed a clear pattern. It might be a bit inconsistent at times, but I couldn’t point to a definite change in my sleep and say that started eleven months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your motivation to do things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say a decrease in motivation exactly. It’s a bit hard to separate out, because with things I normally enjoy, I don’t think I’ve stopped wanting to do them in the sense of not caring about them anymore.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes getting started feels like more of a hurdle than it should, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Oh, I’d like to watch that programme,” or “I should message someone back,” or “It would be nice to do something at the weekend,” but then the thought sits there and I don’t necessarily act on it straight away. But I’m not sure that’s the same as losing motivation or enjoyment.\n\nWhen I actually do end up doing something I like, I can still enjoy it. So I wouldn’t say there’s been a clear loss of interest in things. I haven’t noticed a big change where I suddenly don’t want to do anything I used to like. It’s more… I don’t know, a bit patchy or inconsistent, maybe. But not a definite decrease in motivation, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends or family members due to your difficulties with focusing and following through on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, yes. I think that’s probably one of the areas where it’s become harder to brush off as just me being a bit disorganised.\n\nWith friends and family, it’s not that I’ve fallen out with people exactly, but it has definitely put strain on things. At home especially, my wife has started taking over more of the bills and reminders and general keeping-track-of-life things, because she doesn’t really trust that I’ll remember or follow through consistently. And I don’t blame her, because there have been enough times where I’ve said, “Yes, I’ll sort that,” and then it just hasn’t happened, or I’ve remembered too late, or I’ve needed several reminders. But it still feels horrible, because it puts her in this position of having to manage me a bit, which isn’t fair.\n\nAt work it’s been more formal, I suppose. I’m a secondary school teacher, and the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was quite embarrassing, honestly. It wasn’t one single dramatic thing, more a pattern of things not being completed when I said they would be, or needing chasing, and eventually it became something they had to address properly.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has affected relationships — particularly with my wife, and professionally with colleagues as well. It’s not that people have stopped caring about me, but I can tell they’re more cautious about relying on me, and that’s difficult.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with decision-making or problem-solving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed a very clear problem-solving issue in the sense of, you know, being unable to work things out or not understanding what needs to happen.\n\nDecision-making… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d want to describe it as a major thing. I can be a bit slow to decide if there are lots of small options, or if it feels like I need to pick the “right” way to do something. I might go back and forth in my head for longer than is really necessary. But I don’t know if that’s a separate difficulty or just me overcomplicating things a bit.\n\nWith practical problems, once I’m actually focused on them, I think I can usually solve them reasonably well. It’s more that I might take a while to settle on where to start, or I might leave a decision sitting there because I’m not quite ready to commit to it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly can’t make decisions or solve problems.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. There might be some hesitation or getting stuck on the starting point, but I haven’t really identified it as a distinct problem on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to learn new information?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in any obvious or significant way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I still feel like I can learn new information when I need to. If someone explains something to me properly, or if I sit down and actually engage with it, I can understand it and take it in. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that my memory or ability to learn has suddenly got worse, or that I’m unable to grasp new things.\n\nSometimes I might need to go over something more than once, but I think that’s fairly normal depending on how much is going on or how interested I am in the material. I haven’t had a sense of, “Oh, I used to be able to learn things and now I can’t.” It’s more that some information sticks better than other information, especially if it feels immediately relevant or I’ve properly focused on it.\n\nSo overall, no — I haven’t noticed a significant decline in learning new information.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I’m a secondary school teacher, and the missed follow-through has got serious enough that my department chair has put me on a support plan. That was quite embarrassing, honestly. It wasn’t one single dramatic thing, more a pattern of things not being completed when I said they would be, or needing chasing, and eventually it became something they had to address properly.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has affected relationships — particularly with my wife, and professionally with colleagues as well. It’s not that people have stopped caring about me, but I can tell they’re more cautious about relying on me, and that’s difficult.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties with decision-making or problem-solving?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve noticed a very clear problem-solving issue in the sense of, you know, being unable to work things out or not understanding what needs to happen.\n\nDecision-making… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d want to describe it as a major thing. I can be a bit slow to decide if there are lots of small options, or if it feels like I need to pick the “right” way to do something. I might go back and forth in my head for longer than is really necessary. But I don’t know if that’s a separate difficulty or just me overcomplicating things a bit.\n\nWith practical problems, once I’m actually focused on them, I think I can usually solve them reasonably well. It’s more that I might take a while to settle on where to start, or I might leave a decision sitting there because I’m not quite ready to commit to it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly can’t make decisions or solve problems.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. There might be some hesitation or getting stuck on the starting point, but I haven’t really identified it as a distinct problem on its own.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to learn new information?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in any obvious or significant way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I still feel like I can learn new information when I need to. If someone explains something to me properly, or if I sit down and actually engage with it, I can understand it and take it in. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that my memory or ability to learn has suddenly got worse, or that I’m unable to grasp new things.\n\nSometimes I might need to go over something more than once, but I think that’s fairly normal depending on how much is going on or how interested I am in the material. I haven’t had a sense of, “Oh, I used to be able to learn things and now I can’t.” It’s more that some information sticks better than other information, especially if it feels immediately relevant or I’ve properly focused on it.\n\nSo overall, no — I haven’t noticed a significant decline in learning new information.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any physical symptoms, such as headaches or fatigue, when you're struggling to focus or follow through on tasks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve noticed anything very specific physically, no. Not in the sense that, when I can’t focus, I reliably get a headache or something like that.

I mean, I do sometimes feel worn out after I’ve spent ages trying to force myself through a task, but I’m not sure whether I’d call that a symptom exactly, or just the normal frustration and effort of sitting there pushing against something that isn’t happening. It’s more mental tiredness than anything I can point to physically. I wouldn’t say, “Oh yes, every time I struggle to follow through, I get headaches,” because that doesn’t really ring true.

With fatigue, I suppose it depends what you mean. I can feel drained by the process, especially if I’ve been trying to concentrate for a long time and not getting anywhere, but I haven’t noticed a clear physical pattern that I’d be confident describing. No major headaches or bodily symptoms that stand out to me.
